# f. proof of theorem 6: max clique inlier selection

consider a graph  $\mathcal{g}'(\mathcal{v},\mathcal{e}')$  whose edges where selected as inliers during scale estimation, by pruning the complete graph  $\mathcal{g}(\mathcal{v},\mathcal{e})$ . an edge  $(i,j)$  (and the corresponding tim) is an inlier if both  $i$  and  $j$  are correct correspondences (see discussion before theorem 4). therefore,  $\mathcal{g}'$  contains edges connecting all points for which we have inlier correspondences. therefore, these points are vertices of a clique in the graph  $\mathcal{g}'$  and the edges (or equivalently the tims) connecting those points form a clique in  $\mathcal{g}'$ . we conclude the proof by observing that the clique formed by the inliers has to belong to at least one maximal clique of  $\mathcal{g}'$ .

g. proof of theorem 7: optimal scalar tls estimation

let us first prove that there are at most  $2k - 1$  different nonempty consensus sets. we attach a confidence interval  $[s_k - \alpha_k\bar{c}, s_k + \alpha_k\bar{c}]$  to each measurement  $s_k$ ,  $\forall k \in \{1, \dots, k\}$ . for a given scalar  $s \in \mathbb{r}$ , a measurement  $k$  is in the consensus set of  $s$  if  $s \in [s_k - \alpha_k\bar{c}, s_k + \alpha_k\bar{c}]$  (satisfies  $\frac{\|s - s_k\|^2}{\alpha_k^2} \leq \bar{c}^2$ ), see fig. 3(a). therefore, the only points on the real line where the consensus set may change are the boundaries (shown in red in fig. 3(a)) of the intervals  $[s_k - \alpha_k\bar{c}, s_k + \alpha_k\bar{c}]$ ,  $k \in \{1, \dots, k\}$ . since there are at most  $2k - 1$  such intervals, there are at most  $2k - 1$  non-empty consensus sets (fig. 3(b)), concluding the first part of the proof. the second part follows from the fact that the consensus set of  $\hat{s}$  is necessarily one of the  $2k - 1$  possible consensus sets, and problem (11) simply computes the least squares estimate of the measurements for every possible consensus set (at most  $2k - 1$ ) and chooses the estimate that induces the lowest cost as the optimal estimate.

# h. proof of proposition 9: binary cloning

here we prove the equivalence between the mixed-integer program (17) and the optimization in (18) involving  $n + 1$  quaternions. to do so, we note that since  $\theta_{k} \in \{+1, -1\}$  and  $\frac{1 + \theta_k}{2} \in \{0,1\}$ , we can safely move  $\frac{1 + \theta_k}{2}$  inside the squared norm (because  $0 = 0^2, 1 = 1^2$ ) in each summand of the cost function (17):

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {k} \frac {1 + \theta_ {k}}{2} \frac {\left\| \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} - \boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1} \right\| ^ {2}}{\delta_ {k} ^ {2}} + \frac {1 - \theta_ {k}}{2} \bar {c} ^ {2} \tag {44} \\ = \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {k} \frac {\| \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} - \boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1} + \theta_ {k} \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} - \boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ (\theta_ {k} \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1}) \| ^ {2}}{4 \delta_ {k} ^ {2}} + \frac {1 - \theta_ {k}}{2} \vec {c} ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

now we introduce  $n$  new unit quaternions  $\pmb{q}_k = \theta_k\pmb{q}$ ,  $k = 1,\dots ,n$  by multiplying  $\pmb{q}$  by the  $n$  binary variables  $\theta_{k}\in \{+1, - 1\}$ , a re-parametrization we called binary cloning. one can easily verify that  $\pmb{q}^{\mathrm{t}}\pmb{q}_k = \theta_k(\pmb{q}^{\mathrm{t}}\pmb {q}) = \theta_k$ . hence, by substituting  $\theta_{k} = \pmb{q}^{\mathrm{t}}\pmb{q}_{k}$  into (44), we can rewrite the mixed-integer program (17) as:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min_{\substack{\boldsymbol {q}\in \mathbb{s}^{3}\\ \boldsymbol {q}_{k}\in \{\pm \boldsymbol {q}\}}}\sum_{k = 1}^{k}\frac{\|\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}_{k} - \boldsymbol{q}\circ\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{k}\circ\boldsymbol{q}^{-1} + \boldsymbol{q}^{\top}\boldsymbol{q}_{k}\hat{\boldsymbol{b}}_{k} - \boldsymbol{q}\circ\hat{\boldsymbol{a}}_{k}\circ\boldsymbol{q}_{k}^{-1}\|^{2}}{4\delta_{k}^{2}} \\ + \frac {1 - q ^ {\mathsf {t}} q _ {k}}{2} \bar {c} ^ {2}, \tag {45} \\ \end{array}
$$

which is the same as the optimization in (18).

# i. proof of proposition 10: binary cloning as a qcqp

here we show that the optimization involving  $k + 1$  quaternions in (18) can be reformulated as the quadratically-constrained quadratic program (qcqp) in (19). towards this

goal, we prove that the objective function and the constraints in the qcqp are a re-parametrization of the ones in (18).

equivalence of the objective functions. we start by developing the squared 2-norm term in (18) (we scale  $\bar{a}_k$  by  $\hat{s}\bar{a}_k$ , i.e.,  $\bar{a}_k \doteq \hat{s}\bar{a}_k$  for notation simplicity):

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} - \boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1} + \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} - \boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} ^ {- 1} \right\| ^ {2} \\ \left(\| q ^ {\top} q _ {k} \hat {b} _ {k} \| ^ {2} = \| \hat {b} _ {k} \| ^ {2} = \| \bar {b} _ {k} \| ^ {2}, \hat {b} _ {k} ^ {\top} \left(q ^ {\top} q _ {k}\right) \hat {b} _ {k} = q ^ {\top} q _ {k} \| \bar {b} _ {k} \| ^ {2}\right) \\ \left(\left\| \boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1} \right\| ^ {2} = \left\| \boldsymbol {r} \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \right\| ^ {2} = \left\| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \right\| ^ {2}\right) \\ \left(\| \boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} ^ {- 1} \| ^ {2} = \| \theta_ {k} \boldsymbol {r} \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} = \| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2}\right) \\ \left(\left(q \circ \hat {a} _ {k} \circ q ^ {- 1}\right) ^ {\top} \left(q \circ \hat {a} _ {k} \circ q _ {k} ^ {- 1}\right) = \left(r \bar {a} _ {k}\right) ^ {\top} \left(\theta_ {k} r \bar {a} _ {k}\right) = q ^ {\top} q _ {k} \| \bar {a} _ {k} \| ^ {2}\right) \\ = 2 \| \bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \| \bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ - 2 \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} ^ {\mathsf {t}} (\boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1}) - 2 \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} ^ {\mathsf {t}} (\boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} ^ {- 1}) \\ - 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} ^ {\mathsf {t}} (\boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1}) - 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} ^ {\mathsf {t}} (\boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} ^ {- 1}) (46) \\ \left(q ^ {\top} q _ {k} \hat {b} _ {k} ^ {\top} (q \circ \hat {a} _ {k} \circ q _ {k} ^ {- 1}) = (\theta_ {k}) ^ {2} \hat {b} _ {k} ^ {\top} (q \circ \hat {a} _ {k} \circ q ^ {- 1}) = \hat {b} _ {k} ^ {\top} (q \circ \hat {a} _ {k} \circ q ^ {- 1})\right) \\ \left(\hat {b} _ {k} ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} ^ {- 1}\right) = \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1}\right)\right) \\ = 2 \| \bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \| \bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ - 4 \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} ^ {\mathrm {t}} (\boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1}) - 4 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathrm {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} ^ {\mathrm {t}} (\boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1}) (47) \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have used multiple times the binary cloning equalities  $\pmb{q}_k = \theta_k\pmb{q}, \theta_k = \pmb{q}^\top \pmb{q}_k$ , the equivalence between applying rotation to a homogeneous vector  $\hat{\pmb{a}}_k$  using quaternion product and using rotation matrix in eq. (3) from the main document, as well as the fact that vector 2-norm is invariant to rotation and homogenization (with zero padding).

before moving to the next step, we make the following observation by combining eq. (2) and  $\pmb{q}^{-1} = [-\pmb{v}^{\top}, s]^{\top}$ :

$$
\boldsymbol {\omega} _ {1} \left(\boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1}\right) = \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {1} ^ {\mathsf {t}} (\boldsymbol {q}), \quad \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {2} \left(\boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1}\right) = \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {2} ^ {\mathsf {t}} (\boldsymbol {q}) \tag {48}
$$

which states the linear operators  $\omega_1(\cdot)$  and  $\omega_2(\cdot)$  of  $\mathbf{q}$  and its inverse  $\pmb{q}^{-1}$  are related by a simple transpose operation. in the next step, we use the equivalence between quaternion product and linear operators in  $\omega_1(\pmb{q})$  and  $\omega_2(\pmb{q})$  as defined in eq. (1)-(2) to simplify  $\hat{\pmb{b}}_k^\top (\pmb{q} \circ \hat{\pmb{a}}_k \circ \pmb{q}^{-1})$  in eq. (47):

$$
\hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1})
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left(q \circ \hat {a} _ {k} = \omega_ {1} (q) \hat {a} _ {k}, \omega_ {1} (q) \hat {a} _ {k} \circ q ^ {- 1} = \omega_ {2} (q ^ {- 1}) \omega_ {1} (q) \hat {a} _ {k} = \omega_ {2} ^ {\top} (q) \omega_ {1} (q) \hat {a} _ {k}\right) \\ = \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \left(\boldsymbol {\omega} _ {2} ^ {\mathsf {t}} (\boldsymbol {q}) \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {1} (\boldsymbol {q}) \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k}\right) \tag {49} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left(\omega_ {2} (q) \hat {b} _ {k} = \hat {b} _ {k} \circ q = \omega_ {1} \left(\hat {b} _ {k}\right) q, \omega_ {1} (q) \hat {a} _ {k} = q \circ \hat {a} _ {k} = \omega_ {2} \left(\hat {a} _ {k}\right) q\right) \\ = \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {1} ^ {\top} \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k}\right) \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {2} \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k}\right) \boldsymbol {q}. \tag {50} \\ \end{array}
$$

now we can insert eq. (50) back to eq. (47) and write:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} - \boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1} + \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} - \boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} ^ {- 1} \right\| ^ {2} \\ = 2 \| \bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \| \bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ - 4 \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} ^ {\mathrm {t}} (\boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1}) - 4 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathrm {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} ^ {\mathrm {t}} (\boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1}) \tag {51} \\ = 2 \| \bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \| \bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} - 4 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {1} ^ {\top} (\hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k}) \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {2} (\hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k}) \boldsymbol {q} - 4 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {1} ^ {\top} (\hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k}) \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {2} (\hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k}) \boldsymbol {q} (52) \\ \left(q ^ {\top} q _ {k} q ^ {\top} \omega_ {1} ^ {\top} (\hat {b} _ {k}) \omega_ {2} (\hat {a} _ {k}) q = \theta_ {k} q ^ {\top} \omega_ {1} ^ {\top} (\hat {b} _ {k}) \omega_ {2} (\hat {a} _ {k}) q = q ^ {\top} \omega_ {1} ^ {\top} (\hat {b} _ {k}) \omega_ {2} (\hat {a} _ {k}) q _ {k}\right) \\ = 2 \| \bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \| \bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ - 4 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {1} ^ {\top} (\hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k}) \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {2} (\hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k}) \boldsymbol {q} - 4 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {1} ^ {\top} (\hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k}) \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {2} (\hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k}) \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} (53) \\ \left(- \omega_ {1} ^ {\mathrm {t}} \left(\hat {b} _ {k}\right) = \omega_ {1} \left(\hat {b} _ {k}\right)\right) \\ = 2 \| \bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \| \bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} \\ + 4 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {1} (\hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k}) \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {2} (\hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k}) \boldsymbol {q} + 4 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {1} (\hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k}) \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {2} (\hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k}) \boldsymbol {q} _ {k}, (54) \\ \end{array}
$$

which is quadratic in  $\pmb{q}$  and  $\pmb{q}_k$ . substituting eq. (54) back to (18), we can write the cost function as:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {k} \frac {\left\| \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} - \boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} ^ {- 1} + \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} - \boldsymbol {q} \circ \hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \circ \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} ^ {- 1} \right\| ^ {2}}{4 \delta_ {k} ^ {2}} + \frac {1 - \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k}}{2} \bar {c} ^ {2} \\ = \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {k} q _ {k} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \left(\underbrace {\frac {(\| \bar {b} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \| \bar {a} _ {k} \| ^ {2}) \mathbf {i} _ {4} + 2 \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {1} (\hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k}) \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {2} (\hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k})}{2 \delta_ {k} ^ {2}} + \frac {\bar {c} ^ {2}}{2} \mathbf {i} _ {4}} _ {:= q _ {k k}}\right) q _ {k} \\ + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \left(\underbrace {\frac {\left(\| \bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k} \| ^ {2}\right) \mathbf {i} _ {4} + 2 \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {1} \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k}\right) \boldsymbol {\omega} _ {2} \left(\hat {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k}\right)}{4 \delta_ {k} ^ {2}} - \frac {\bar {c} ^ {2}}{4} \mathbf {i} _ {4}} _ {:= q _ {0 k}}\right) \boldsymbol {q} _ {k}, \tag {55} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we have used two facts: (i)  $\pmb{q}^{\mathsf{t}}\pmb {a}\pmb {q} = \theta_{k}^{2}\pmb{q}^{\mathsf{t}}\pmb {a}\pmb {q} =$ $q_{k}^{\top}aq_{k}$  for any matrix  $\pmb {a}\in \mathbb{r}^{4\times 4}$  (kk)  $c = c\pmb{q}^{\top}\pmb {q} = \pmb{q}^{\top}(c\mathbf{i}_{4})\pmb{q}$  for any real constant  $c$  , which allowed writing the quadratic forms of  $\pmb{q}$  and constant terms in the cost as quadratic forms of  $\pmb{q}_k$  . since we have not changed the decision variables  $\pmb{q}$  and  $\{q_k\}_{k = 1}^k$  , the optimization in (18) is therefore equivalent to the following optimization:

$$
\min  _ {\substack {\boldsymbol {q} \in s ^ {3} \\ \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \in \{\pm \boldsymbol {q} \}}} \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {k} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k k} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} _ {0 k} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \tag{56}
$$

where  $q_{kk}$  and  $q_{0k}$  are the known  $4 \times 4$  data matrices as defined in eq. (55).

now it remains to prove that the above optimization (56) is equivalent to the qcqp in (19). recall that  $\pmb{x}$  is the column vector stacking all the  $k + 1$  quaternions, i.e.,  $\pmb{x} = [q^{\mathsf{t}}q_{1}^{\mathsf{t}}\dots q_{n}^{\mathsf{t}}]^{\mathsf{t}}\in \mathbb{r}^{4(k + 1)}$ . let us introduce symmetric matrices  $\pmb{q}_k\in \mathbb{r}^{4(k + 1)\times 4(k + 1)},k = 1,\ldots ,k$  and let the  $4\times 4$  sub-block of  $\pmb{q}_k$  corresponding to sub-vector  $\pmb{u}$  and  $\pmb{v}$ , be denoted as  $[\pmb{q}_k]_{uv}$ ; each  $\pmb{q}_k$  is defined as:

$$
\left[ \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \right] _ {u v} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k k} & \text {i f} \boldsymbol {u} = \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \text {a n d} \boldsymbol {v} = \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \\ \boldsymbol {q} _ {0 k} & \text {i f} \boldsymbol {u} = \boldsymbol {q} \text {a n d} \boldsymbol {v} = \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \\ 0 _ {4 \times 4} & \text {o r} \boldsymbol {u} = \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \text {a n d} \boldsymbol {v} = \boldsymbol {q} \end{array} \right. \tag {57}
$$

i.e.,  $\pmb{q}_k$  has the diagonal  $4 \times 4$  sub-block corresponding to  $(\pmb{q}_k, \pmb{q}_k)$  be  $\pmb{q}_{kk}$ , has the two off-diagonal  $4 \times 4$  sub-blocks corresponding to  $(\pmb{q}, \pmb{q}_k)$  and  $(\pmb{q}_k, \pmb{q})$  be  $\pmb{q}_{0k}$ , and has all the other  $4 \times 4$  sub-blocks be zero. then we can write the cost function in eq. (56) compactly using  $\pmb{x}$  and  $\pmb{q}_k$ :

$$
\sum_ {k = 1} ^ {k} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k k} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} + 2 \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} _ {0 k} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} = \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {k} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \boldsymbol {x} \tag {58}
$$

therefore, by denoting  $\mathbf{q} = \sum_{k=1}^{k} \mathbf{q}_k$ , we proved that the objective functions in (18) and the qcqp (19) are the same.

equivalence of the constraints. we are only left to prove that (18) and (19) have the same feasible set, i.e., the following two sets of constraints are equivalent:

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {q} \in \mathbb {s} ^ {3} \\ \boldsymbol {q} _ {k} \in \{\pm \boldsymbol {q} \}, \\ k = 1, \dots , k \end{array} \right. \leftrightarrow \left\{ \begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {x} _ {q} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {x} _ {q} = 1 \\ \boldsymbol {x} _ {q _ {k}} \boldsymbol {x} _ {q _ {k}} ^ {\top} = \boldsymbol {x} _ {q} \boldsymbol {x} _ {q} ^ {\top}, \\ k = 1, \dots , k \end{array} \right. \tag {59}
$$

we first prove the  $(\rightarrow)$  direction. since  $\pmb{q} \in \mathbb{s}^3$ , it is obvious that  $\pmb{x}_q^\top \pmb{x}_q = \pmb{q}^\top \pmb{q} = 1$ . in addition, since  $\pmb{q}_k \in \{+q, -q\}$ , it follows that  $\pmb{x}_{q_k}\pmb{x}_{q_k}^\top = \pmb{q}_k\pmb{q}_k^\top = \pmb{q}\pmb{q}^\top = \pmb{x}_q\pmb{x}_q^\top$ . then we proof the reverse direction  $(\leftarrow)$ . since  $\pmb{x}_q^\top \pmb{x}_q = \pmb{q}^\top \pmb{q}$ , so  $\pmb{x}_q^\top \pmb{x}_q = 1$  implies  $\pmb{q}^\top \pmb{q} = 1$  and therefore  $\pmb{q} \in \mathbb{s}^3$ . on the other hand,  $\pmb{x}_{q_k}\pmb{x}_{q_k}^\top = \pmb{x}_q\pmb{x}_q^\top$  means  $\pmb{q}_k\pmb{q}_k^\top = \pmb{q}\pmb{q}^\top$ . if we write  $\pmb{q}_k = [q_{k1}, q_{k2}, q_{k3}, q_{k4}]^\top$  and  $\pmb{q} = [q_1, q_2, q_3, q_4]$ , then the following matrix equality holds:

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} q _ {k 1} ^ {2} & q _ {k 1} q _ {k 2} & q _ {k 1} q _ {k 3} & q _ {k 1} q _ {k 4} \\ \star & q _ {k 2} ^ {2} & q _ {k 2} q _ {k 3} & q _ {k 2} q _ {k 4} \\ \star & \star & q _ {k 3} ^ {2} & q _ {k 3} q _ {k 4} \\ \star & \star & \star & q _ {k 4} ^ {2} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} q _ {1} ^ {2} & q _ {1} q _ {2} & q _ {1} q _ {3} & q _ {1} q _ {4} \\ \star & q _ {2} ^ {2} & q _ {2} q _ {3} & q _ {2} q _ {4} \\ \star & \star & q _ {3} ^ {2} & q _ {3} q _ {4} \\ \star & \star & \star & q _ {4} ^ {2} \end{array} \right] \tag {60}
$$

first, from the diagonal equalities, we can get  $q_{ki} = \theta_i q_i, \theta_i \in \{+1, -1\}, i = 1, 2, 3, 4$ . then we look at the off-diagonal equality:  $q_{ki} q_{kj} = q_i q_j, i \neq j$ , since  $q_{ki} = \theta_i q_i$  and  $q_{kj} = \theta_j q_j$ , we have  $q_{ki} q_{kj} = \theta_i \theta_j q_j q_k$ , from which we obtain  $\theta_i \theta_j = 1, \forall i \neq j$ . this implies that all the binary values  $\{\theta_i\}_{i=1}^4$  have the same sign, and therefore they are equal to each other. as a result,  $\mathbf{q}_k = \theta_k \mathbf{q} = \{+q, -q\}$ , showing the two sets of constraints in eq. (59) are indeed equivalent. therefore, the qcqp in eq. (19) is equivalent to the optimization in (56), and the original optimization in (18) that involves  $k + 1$  quaternions, concluding the proof.

# j. proof of proposition 11: matrix formulation of binary cloning

here we show that the non-convex qcqp written in terms of the vector  $\mathbf{x}$  in proposition 10 (and eq. (19)) is equivalent to the non-convex problem written using the matrix  $z$  in proposition 11 (and eq. (22)). we do so by showing that the objective function and the constraints in (22) are a reparametrization of the ones in (19).

equivalence of the objective function. since  $z = xx^{\top}$  and using the cyclic property of the trace, we rewrite the objective in (19) as:

$$
\boldsymbol {x} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {x} = \operatorname {t r} \left(\boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\mathsf {t}}\right) = \operatorname {t r} (\boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {z}) \tag {61}
$$

showing the equivalence of the objectives in (19) and (22).

equivalence of the constraints. it is trivial to see that  $\boldsymbol{x}_q^\top \boldsymbol{x}_q = \operatorname{tr}(\boldsymbol{x}_q \boldsymbol{x}_q^\top) = 1$  is equivalent to  $\operatorname{tr}([\boldsymbol{z}]_{qq}) = 1$  by using the cyclic property of the trace operator and inspecting the structure of  $z$ . in addition,  $\boldsymbol{x}_{q_k} \boldsymbol{x}_{q_k}^\top = \boldsymbol{x}_q \boldsymbol{x}_q^\top$  also directly maps to  $[\boldsymbol{z}]_{q_k q_k} = [\boldsymbol{z}]_{qq}$  for all  $i = 1, \ldots, k$ . lastly, requiring  $z \succeq 0$  and  $\operatorname{rank}(z) = 1$  is equivalent to restricting  $z$  to the form  $z = x x^\top$  for some vector  $x \in \mathbb{r}^{4(k+1)}$ . therefore, the constraint sets of eq. (19) and (22) are also equivalent, concluding the proof.

# k. proof of theorem 13: sdp relaxation with redundant constraints and global optimality guarantee

we first restate the qcqp (19) and explicitly add the redundant constraints:

lemma 24 (qcqp with redundant constraints). the qcqp (19) is equivalent to the following qcqp with redundant constraints:

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {x} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {4 (k + 1)}} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {x} \tag {p}
$$

$$
s. t. \boldsymbol {x} _ {q} ^ {\intercal} \boldsymbol {x} _ {q} = 1,
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {x} _ {q _ {k}} \boldsymbol {x} _ {q _ {k}} ^ {\mathsf {t}} = \boldsymbol {x} _ {q} \boldsymbol {x} _ {q} ^ {\mathsf {t}}, \forall k = 1, \dots , k,
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {x} _ {q _ {i}} \boldsymbol {x} _ {q _ {j}} ^ {\intercal} = (\boldsymbol {x} _ {q _ {i}} \boldsymbol {x} _ {q _ {j}} ^ {\intercal}) ^ {\intercal}, \forall 0 \leq i <   j \leq k,
$$

where  $\mathbf{q}_0 \doteq \mathbf{q}$  for notation simplicity.

the redundancy of the last set of constraints can be easily understood from:

$$
\boldsymbol {x} _ {q _ {i}} \boldsymbol {x} _ {q _ {j}} ^ {\top} = \left(\theta_ {i} \boldsymbol {q}\right) \left(\theta_ {j} \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top}\right) = \left(\theta_ {j} \boldsymbol {q}\right) \left(\theta_ {i} \boldsymbol {q} ^ {\top}\right) = \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {q _ {i}} \boldsymbol {x} _ {q _ {j}} ^ {\top}\right) ^ {\top}. \tag {62}
$$

since the original qcqp (19) is equivalent to the rank-constrained sdp (22), problem (p) is also equivalent to (22).

now let us prove theorem 13. while the sdp relaxation of (p) can be simply obtained by adding the redundant constraints and dropping the rank constraint in (22). here we take a longer path, using lagrangian duality, which is useful towards getting the guarantees stated in the theorem.

lemma 25 (dual of qcqp with redundant constraints). the lagrangian dual problem of the qcqp with redundant constraints (p) is the following convex semidefinite program:

$$
\max  _ {\mu , \lambda , w} \mu \tag {d}
$$

$$
s. t. \left(\boldsymbol {q} - \mu \boldsymbol {j} + \boldsymbol {\lambda} + \boldsymbol {w}\right) \succeq 0,
$$

where  $\pmb{j} \in s^{4(k + 1)}$  is an all-zero matrix except the top-left diagonal  $4 \times 4$  block  $[j]_{00} = \mathbf{i}_4$ ;  $\pmb{\lambda} \in s^{4(k + 1)}$  is a block-diagonal matrix  $\pmb{\lambda} = \text{blkdiag}(\pmb{\lambda}_{00}, \pmb{\lambda}_{11}, \dots, \pmb{\lambda}_{kk})$ , where each  $\pmb{\lambda}_{kk}$  is a  $4 \times 4$  symmetric matrix and the sum  $\sum_{k=0}^{k} \pmb{\lambda}_{kk} = \mathbf{0}$ ; and  $\pmb{w} \in s^{4(k + 1)}$  satisfies  $[\pmb{w}]_{ij} = \pmb{0}$  for  $i = j$  and  $[\pmb{w}]_{ij} \in \mathfrak{s}^4$  (i.e.,  $[\pmb{w}]_{ij}$  is a skew symmetric matrix) for  $i \neq j$ .

the dual problem (d) is derived from the redundant qcqp (p) by following standard lagrangian duality [108]. for any  $x$  that is feasible for (p), one can verify that:

$$
\boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {j} \boldsymbol {x} = 1, \quad \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {x} = 0, \quad \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {w} \boldsymbol {x} = 0. \tag {63}
$$

lemma 26 (dual of the dual). the dual sdp of the dual problem (d) is the following sdp:

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {z} \succeq 0} \operatorname {t r} (\boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {z}) \tag {dd}
$$

$$
s. t. \operatorname {t r} ([ z ] _ {0 0}) = 1
$$

$$
[ \boldsymbol {z} ] _ {k k} = [ \boldsymbol {z} ] _ {0 0}, \forall k = 1, \dots , k
$$

$$
[ \boldsymbol {z} ] _ {i j} = [ \boldsymbol {z} ] _ {i j} ^ {\mathsf {t}}, \forall 0 \leq i <   j \leq k,
$$

which is the same as the sdp relaxation with redundant constraints (23).

now we are ready to prove theorem 13. first of all, we have the following weak duality:

$$
f _ {d} ^ {\star} = f _ {d d} ^ {\star} \leq f _ {p} ^ {\star}, \tag {64}
$$

where  $f_{d}^{\star} = f_{dd}^{\star}$  (strong duality of the sdp pair) is due to the fact that (dd) has a strictly feasible solution, by having  $[\mathbf{z}]_{00} = [\mathbf{z}]_{11} = \dots = [\mathbf{z}]_{kk} = \frac{1}{4}\mathbf{i}_4$  and  $[\mathbf{z}]_{ij} = \mathbf{0}$  for  $i \neq j$  (in this case  $\mathbf{z} = \frac{1}{4}\mathbf{i}_{4(k + 1)} \succ 0$ ), and  $f_{dd}^{\star} \leq f_{p}^{\star}$  is due to the fact that (dd) is a convex relaxation for (p).

if the optimal solution of (dd) satisfies  $\mathrm{rank}(z^{\star}) = 1$ , then this means that  $z^{\star}$  is also feasible for the rank-constrained sdp (22). therefore, the minimum  $f_{dd}^{\star}$  can indeed be attained by a point in the feasible set of problem (22). because the rank-constrained problem (22) is equivalent to (p),  $f_{dd}^{\star} = f_{p}^{\star}$  must hold and the rank-1 decomposition of  $z^{\star}$ ,  $x^{\star}$  must be a global minimizer of problem (p).

to prove the uniqueness of  $\pm x^{\star}$  as global minimizers of (p), denote  $\mu^{\star} = (\pmb{x}^{\star})^{\top}q(\pmb{x}^{\star}) = \mathrm{tr}(qz^{\star})$  as the global minimum of (p), (d) and (dd), and  $\lambda^{\star}, w^{\star}$  as the corresponding dual variables. let  $m^{\star} = q - \mu^{\star}j + \lambda^{\star} + w^{\star}$ , we have  $\mathrm{rank}(m^{\star}) = 4(k + 1) - 1$  and its kernel is  $\ker(m^{\star}) = \{x : x = ax^{\star}\}$  from complementary slackness of the pair of sdp duals. denote the feasible set of (p) as  $\omega(p)$ , then we have  $\ker(m^{\star}) \cap \omega(p) = \pm x^{\star}$ . therefore, for any  $x \in \omega(p) / \{\pm x^{\star}\}$ , we have:

$$
\boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} m ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {x} > 0 \tag {65}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \leftrightarrow \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {x} - \mu^ {\star} + \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {x} + \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\star} \boldsymbol {x} > 0 (66) \\ \leftrightarrow \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {x} > \mu^ {\star}, (67) \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\pmb{x}^{\top}\pmb{\lambda}^{\star}\pmb{x} = \pmb{x}^{\top}\pmb{w}^{\star}\pmb{x} = 0$  holds due to (63).

# l. proof of theorem 14: optimality certification

we prove theorem 14 in three steps. first, we show that that proving global optimality of an estimate for the rotation estimation problem (15) relates to the existence of a matrix at the intersection of the positive semidefinite (psd) cone and an affine subspace (appendix l1). second, we show that we can compute a sub-optimality gap (this is the bound  $\eta$  in the theorem) for an estimate using any matrix in the affine subspace (appendix l2). finally, we show that under the conditions of theorem 14 such bound converges to zero when the provided estimate is globally optimal (appendix l3).

1) matrix certificate for global optimality: here we derive necessary and sufficient conditions for global optimality of the tls rotation estimation problem (15).

theorem 27 (sufficient condition for global optimality). given a feasible solution  $(\hat{q},\hat{\theta}_1,\dots ,\hat{\theta}_k)$  of the tls rotation estimation problem (15), denote  $\hat{\pmb{x}} = [\hat{\pmb{q}}^{\mathsf{t}},\hat{\theta}_{1}\hat{\pmb{q}}^{\mathsf{t}},\dots ,\hat{\theta}_{k}\hat{\pmb{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}]^{\mathsf{t}}$  and  $\hat{\mu} = \hat{\pmb{x}}^{\mathsf{t}}\pmb {q}\hat{\pmb{x}}$  as the corresponding solution and cost of the qcqp (p). then  $\hat{\mu}$  (resp.  $(\hat{q},\hat{\theta}_1,\dots ,\hat{\theta}_k)$ ) is the global minimum (resp. minimizer) of problem (15) if there exists a matrix  $m$  that satisfies the following:

$$
m \in \mathcal {l}, \tag {68}
$$

$$
m \succeq 0, \tag {69}
$$

where  $\mathcal{l}$  is the following affine subspace:

$$
\mathcal {l} \doteq \left\{m \in \mathcal {s} ^ {4 (k + 1)} \middle | \begin{array}{l} m \hat {\boldsymbol {x}} = \boldsymbol {0}, \\ m - q + \hat {\mu} j \in \mathcal {h}. \end{array} \right\} \tag {70}
$$

and  $\mathcal{h}$  is defined as:

$$
\mathcal {h} \doteq \left\{\boldsymbol {\delta} \in \mathcal {s} ^ {4 (n + 1)} \bigg | \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {k} [ \boldsymbol {\delta} ] _ {k k} = \mathbf {0}, \right. \tag {71}
$$

the matrix  $\mathbf{j} \in s^{4(k + 1)}$  is an all-zero matrix except the top-left  $4 \times 4$  block  $[\mathbf{j}]_{00} = \mathbf{i}_4$  (the same defined in appendix k).

proof: because  $m - q + \hat{\mu} j \in \mathcal{h}$ , and from the definition of  $\mathcal{h}$ , we can write  $m$  as:

$$
\boldsymbol {m} = \boldsymbol {q} - \hat {\mu} \boldsymbol {j} + \boldsymbol {\lambda} + \boldsymbol {w}, \tag {72}
$$

where  $\lambda, w \in s^{4(k + 1)}$  are the dual variables defined in lemma 25. from  $m \succeq 0$ , we have the following inequality for any  $x$  that is primal feasible for (p):

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {x} \geq 0 \leftrightarrow \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\mathsf {t}} (\boldsymbol {q} - \hat {\mu} \boldsymbol {j} + \boldsymbol {\lambda} + \boldsymbol {w}) \boldsymbol {x} \geq 0 (73) \\ \leftrightarrow \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {x} \geq \hat {\mu} + \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {\lambda} \boldsymbol {x} + \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {w} \boldsymbol {x} = \hat {\mu}, (74) \\ \end{array}
$$

which states that  $\hat{\mu}$  (resp.  $\hat{x}$ ) is the global minimum (resp. minimizer) of (p).

in addition, denote  $\hat{z} = \hat{x}\hat{x}^{\top}$ , then  $\hat{z}$  and  $(\hat{\mu},\lambda ,w)$  are a pair of primal-dual optimal solutions for (dd) and (d), which leads to the following sdp complementary slackness condition:

$$
\left(\boldsymbol {q} - \hat {\mu} \boldsymbol {j} + \boldsymbol {\lambda} + \boldsymbol {w}\right) \hat {\boldsymbol {z}} = \mathbf {0} \tag {75}
$$

$$
\leftrightarrow (\boldsymbol {q} - \hat {\mu} \boldsymbol {j} + \boldsymbol {\lambda} + \boldsymbol {w}) \hat {\boldsymbol {x}} = \mathbf {0}. \tag {76}
$$

therefore,  $m\hat{\pmb{x}} = \mathbf{0}$  must hold in eq. (68).

corollary 28 (sufficient and necessary condition for global optimality). if strong duality is achieved, i.e.,  $f_{d}^{\star} = f_{dd}^{\star} = f_{p}^{\star}$  is attained, then the existence of a matrix  $m$  that satisfies eq. (68) and (69) is also a necessary condition for  $\hat{\mu}$  (resp.  $(\hat{q},\hat{\theta}_1,\dots ,\hat{\theta}_k)$ ) to be the global minimum (resp. minimizer) of problem (15).

proof: if strong duality is achieved, then there must exist dual variables  $\pmb{\lambda}$  and  $\pmb{w}$  that satisfy  $q - \hat{\mu} j + \lambda + w \succeq 0$ . the rest follows from the proof of theorem 27.

2) sub-optimality bound: theorem 27 suggests that finding a matrix  $m$  that lies at the intersection of the psd cone  $s_{+}$  and the affine subspace  $\mathcal{l}$  gives a certificate for  $\hat{x}$  to be globally optimal for (p). corollary 29 below states that even if  $\hat{x}$  is not a globally optimal solution, finding any  $m \in \mathcal{l}$  (not necessarily psd) still gives a valid sub-optimality bound using the minimum eigenvalue of  $m$ .<sup>10</sup>

corollary 29 (relative sub-optimality bound). given a feasible solution  $(\hat{q},\hat{\theta}_1,\dots ,\hat{\theta}_k)$  of the tls rotation estimation problem (15), denote  $\hat{\pmb{x}} = [\hat{\pmb{q}}^{\mathsf{t}},\hat{\theta}_{1}\hat{\pmb{q}}^{\mathsf{t}},\dots ,\hat{\theta}_{k}\hat{\pmb{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}]^{\mathsf{t}}$  and  $\hat{\mu} = \hat{\pmb{x}}^{\mathsf{t}}\pmb {q}\hat{\pmb{x}}$  as the corresponding solution and cost of the qcqp (p). define the affine subspace  $\mathcal{l}$  as in (70). for any  $m\in \mathcal{l}$ , let  $\lambda_{1}(m)$  be its minimum eigenvalue. then the relative sub-optimality gap of  $\hat{\pmb{x}}$  is bounded by:

$$
\frac {\hat {\mu} - \mu^ {\star}}{\hat {\mu}} \leq \eta \doteq \frac {| \lambda_ {1} (m) | (k + 1)}{\hat {\mu}}, \tag {77}
$$

<sup>10</sup>note that the expression of  $\mathcal{l}$  depends on the estimate  $\hat{\pmb{x}}$  as per (70).

where  $\mu^{\star}$  is the true global minimum of (p).

proof: for any  $\pmb{x}$  that is primal feasible for (p), we have  $\| \pmb{x} \|^2 = \sum_{k=1}^{k} \| \pmb{q}_k \|^2 = k + 1$ . therefore, for any  $\pmb{x}$  in the feasible set of (p) and  $m \in \mathcal{l}$ , the following holds:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \boldsymbol {m} \boldsymbol {x} \geq \lambda_ {1} (\boldsymbol {m}) \| \boldsymbol {x} \| ^ {2} = \lambda_ {1} (\boldsymbol {m}) (k + 1) (78) \\ \stackrel {(i)} {\leftrightarrow} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {q} - \hat {\mu} \boldsymbol {j} + \boldsymbol {\lambda} + \boldsymbol {w}) \boldsymbol {x} \geq \lambda_ {1} (\boldsymbol {m}) (k + 1) (79) \\ \stackrel {(i i)} {\leftrightarrow} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {q} \boldsymbol {x} \geq \hat {\mu} + \lambda_ {1} (\boldsymbol {m}) (k + 1). (80) \\ \end{array}
$$

where (i) follows from the fact that any  $m \in \mathcal{l}$  can be written as  $q - \hat{\mu} j + \lambda + w$  (see eq. (72)), and (ii) follows from (63). since  $\mu^{\star}$  is also achieved in the feasible set, eq. (80) also holds for  $\mu^{\star}$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mu^ {\star} \geq \hat {\mu} + \lambda_ {1} (m) (k + 1) (81) \\ \leftrightarrow \frac {\hat {\mu} - \mu^ {\star}}{\hat {\mu}} \leq \frac {| \lambda_ {1} (m) | (k + 1)}{\hat {\mu}}, (82) \\ \end{array}
$$

where in eq. (82) we have used  $\lambda_1(m) \leq 0$  since  $m$  has a zero eigenvalue with eigenvector  $\hat{\pmb{x}}$  ( $m\hat{\pmb{x}} = \mathbf{0}$  in eq. (68)).

3) douglas-rachford splitting and algorithm 3: theorems 27-28 relate global optimality to the existence of a matrix  $m \in s_{+} \cap \mathcal{l}$ , while theorem 29 obtains a suboptimality bound from any matrix  $m \in \mathcal{l}$ . the next theorem states that finding a matrix  $m \in s_{+} \cap \mathcal{l}$  is equivalent to finding a matrix  $\bar{m} \in s_{+} \cap \bar{\mathcal{l}}$ , where  $\bar{\mathcal{l}}$  is a "rotated" version of  $\mathcal{l}$  (this will enable faster projections to this subspace).

theorem 30 (rotated affine subspace). given a feasible solution  $(\hat{\boldsymbol{q}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_1,\dots ,\hat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_k)$  of the tls rotation estimation problem (15), define the block-diagonal matrix  $\hat{\omega}_q = \mathbf{i}_{k + 1}\otimes$ $\omega_{1}(\hat{q})$  . then a matrix  $m\in \mathcal{l}\cap s_{+}$  if and only if  $\bar{m}\doteq$ $\hat{\omega}_q^\top m\hat{\omega}_q\in \bar{\mathcal{l}}\cap s_+$  with  $\bar{\mathcal{l}}$  defined as:

$$
\bar {\mathcal {l}} \doteq \left\{\bar {m} \in \mathcal {s} ^ {4 (k + 1)} \big | \begin{array}{c} \bar {m} \bar {\boldsymbol {x}} = \mathbf {0}, \\ \bar {m} - \bar {q} + \hat {\mu} j \in \mathcal {h}. \end{array} \right\}, \tag {83}
$$

where  $\bar{\pmb{x}} = [1,\hat{\theta}_1,\dots ,\hat{\theta}_k]^{\top}\otimes \pmb{e}$  (with  $\pmb {e} = [0,0,0,1]^{\top}$ ) and  $\bar{\pmb{q}} = \hat{\omega}_{q}^{\top}\pmb {q}\hat{\omega}_{q}$ . moreover,  $\tilde{m}$  and  $m$  produce the same relative sub-optimality bound in theorem 29.

proof: using the expression of  $\omega_1(\pmb{q})$  in (2), one can verify that  $\omega_{1}(\pmb{q})^{\mathsf{t}}\pmb{q} = \pmb{e}$ ,  $\hat{\omega}_q^\top \hat{x} = \bar{x}$  and  $\hat{\omega}_q^\top \hat{\omega}_q = \hat{\omega}_q\hat{\omega}_q^\top = \mathbf{i}_{4(k + 1)}$ . therefore, we have the following equivalence:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \bar {m} \bar {\boldsymbol {x}} = \mathbf {0} \leftrightarrow \hat {\omega} _ {q} ^ {\top} m \hat {\omega} _ {q} \hat {\omega} _ {q} ^ {\top} \hat {\boldsymbol {x}} = \mathbf {0} \\ \leftrightarrow \hat {\omega} _ {q} m \hat {\boldsymbol {x}} = \mathbf {0} \leftrightarrow m \hat {\boldsymbol {x}} = \mathbf {0}. \tag {84} \\ \end{array}
$$

in addition, it is easy to verify that  $\pmb{\delta} \in \mathcal{h}$  if and only if  $\bar{\pmb{\delta}} \doteq \hat{\pmb{\omega}}_q^\top \pmb{\delta} \hat{\pmb{\omega}}_q \in \mathcal{h}$ , and  $\hat{\pmb{\omega}}_q^\top \pmb{j} \hat{\pmb{\omega}}_q = \pmb{j}$ . therefore,

$$
\boldsymbol {m} - \boldsymbol {q} - \hat {\mu} \boldsymbol {j} \in \mathcal {h} \leftrightarrow \bar {\boldsymbol {m}} - \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} - \hat {\mu} \boldsymbol {j} \in \mathcal {h}, \tag {85}
$$

and we conclude that  $m \in \mathcal{l} \leftrightarrow \bar{m} \in \bar{\mathcal{l}}$ . finally,  $\bar{m} = \hat{\omega}_q^\top m\hat{\omega}_q = \hat{\omega}_q^{-1}m\hat{\omega}_q$  is a similarity transform on  $m$  and shares the same eigenvalues with  $m$ , which trivially leads to  $m \in s_+ \leftrightarrow \bar{m} \in s_+$ . in addition, since  $m$  and  $\bar{m}$  share the same eigenvalues, we have  $\lambda_1(\bar{m}) = \lambda_1(m)$  which means that  $\bar{m}$  and  $m$  produces the same relative suboptimality bound in corollary 29.

therefore, to certify optimality of an estimate, we have to search for a matrix  $\bar{m} \in \bar{\mathcal{l}} \cap s_{+}$ , i.e., at the intersection between two convex sets. although finding a point in the intersection of two convex sets can be expensive (e.g., finding  $\bar{m} \in \bar{\mathcal{l}} \cap s_{+}$  is equivalent to solving a feasibility sdp), the following lemma states that one can find a point in the intersection of two convex sets by cleverly leveraging the individual projections onto the two convex sets, a method that is commonly known as douglas-rachford splitting (drs) [112], [35], [122].

lemma 31 (douglas-rachford splitting). let  $\mathcal{x}_1, \mathcal{x}_2$  be two closed convex sets in the hilbert space  $\mathcal{x}$ , and  $\pi_{\mathcal{x}_1}, \pi_{\mathcal{x}_2}$  be their corresponding projection maps, then for any  $x_0 \in \mathcal{x}$ , the following iterates:

$$
(i) \boldsymbol {x} _ {\tau} ^ {\mathcal {x} _ {1}} = \pi_ {\mathcal {x} _ {1}} (\boldsymbol {x} _ {\tau}), \tag {86}
$$

$$
\left(i i\right) \boldsymbol {x} _ {\tau} ^ {\mathcal {x} _ {2}} = \pi_ {\mathcal {x} _ {2}} \left(2 \boldsymbol {x} _ {\tau} ^ {\mathcal {x} _ {1}} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\tau}\right), \tag {87}
$$

$$
(i i i) \boldsymbol {x} _ {\tau + 1} = \boldsymbol {x} _ {\tau} + \gamma \left(\boldsymbol {x} _ {\tau} ^ {\mathcal {x} _ {2}} - \boldsymbol {x} _ {\tau} ^ {\mathcal {x} _ {1}}\right), \tag {88}
$$

generates a sequence  $\{\pmb{x}_{\tau}\}_{\tau \geq 0}$  that converges to the intersection of  $\mathcal{x}_1$  and  $\mathcal{x}_2$  when  $0 < \gamma < 2$ , provided that the intersection is non-empty.

therefore, the drs (line 11-line 13) in algorithm 3 guarantee to converge to a matrix  $\bar{m} \in \bar{\mathcal{l}} \cap s_{+}$  when  $\bar{\mathcal{l}} \cap s_{+} \neq \emptyset$ , and the sub-optimality bound  $\eta$  converges to zero. moreover, algorithm 3 produces a sub-optimality bound even if it fails to certify global optimality, thanks to corollary 29.

# m. closed-form projections

although lemma 31 is theoretically sound, in practice computing the projection onto an arbitrary convex set could be computationally expensive<sup>11</sup>. however, we will show that both projections,  $\pi_{\bar{\mathcal{l}}}$  and  $\pi_{s_+}$  in algorithm 3 can be computed efficiently in closed form.

the projection onto the psd cone is presented in the following lemma due to higham [111].

lemma 32 (projection onto  $s_{+}^{n}$ ). given any matrix  $m \in s^{n}$ , let  $m = u\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_1, \ldots, \lambda_n)u^{\mathsf{t}}$  be its spectral decomposition, then the projection of  $m$  onto the psd cone  $s_{+}^{n}$  is:

$$
\pi_ {\mathcal {s} _ {+} ^ {n}} (\boldsymbol {m}) = \boldsymbol {u} \operatorname {d i a g} \left(\max  \left(0, \lambda_ {1}\right), \dots , \max  \left(0, \lambda_ {n}\right)\right) \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top}. \tag {89}
$$

now let us focus on the affine projection onto  $\bar{\mathcal{l}}$ . the expression of the projection  $\pi_{\bar{\mathcal{l}}}$  is given in proposition 33 below. before stating the result, let us introduce some notation. for two  $4 \times 4$  matrices  $x \in s^4$  and  $y \in \mathfrak{s}^4$ , we partition  $x$  and  $y$  as:

$$
\boldsymbol {x} = \left[ \begin{array}{l l} \boldsymbol {x} ^ {m} \in \mathcal {s} ^ {3} & \boldsymbol {x} ^ {v} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {3} \\ (\boldsymbol {x} ^ {v}) ^ {\top} & \boldsymbol {x} ^ {s} \in \mathbb {r} \end{array} \right], \boldsymbol {y} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \boldsymbol {y} ^ {m} \in \mathfrak {s} ^ {3} & \boldsymbol {y} ^ {v} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {3} \\ - (\boldsymbol {y} ^ {v}) ^ {\top} & \boldsymbol {y} ^ {s} = 0 \end{array} \right], \tag {90}
$$

where the superscripts  $m, v, s$  denotes the top-left  $3 \times 3$  matrix part, the top-right  $3 \times 1$  vector part and the bottom-right scalar part, respectively. for a matrix  $m \in s^{4(k + 1)}$ , we partition it into  $(k + 1)^2$  blocks of size  $4 \times 4$ , and we create a set of

<sup>11</sup>even for an affine subspace,  $\mathcal{a} = \{\pmb{x} \in \mathbb{r}^n : \pmb{a}\pmb{x} = \pmb{b}\}$ , the naive projection of any  $\pmb{x}$  onto  $\mathcal{a}$  is:  $\pi_{\mathcal{a}}(\pmb{x}) = \pmb{x} - \pmb{a}^{\top}(\pmb{a}\pmb{a}^{\top})^{-1}(\pmb{a}\pmb{x} - \pmb{b})$ , which could be expensive for large  $n$  due to the computation of the inverse  $(\pmb{a}\pmb{a}^{\top})^{-1}$ .

$l \doteq \frac{k(k + 1)}{2}$  ordered indices (left to right, top to bottom) that enumerate the upper-triangular off-diagonal blocks:

$$
\mathcal {z} = \left\{(0, 1), \dots , (0, k), (1, 2), \dots , (1, k), \dots , (k - 1, k) \right\}, \tag {91}
$$

where each  $[m]_{\mathcal{z}(l)}, l = 1, \ldots, l$ , takes a  $4 \times 4$  off-diagonal block from  $m$ . then, we define a linear map  $\mathcal{c}: \mathcal{s}^{4(k + 1)} \to \mathbb{r}^{l \times 3}$  that assembles the top-right  $3 \times 1$  vector part of each upper-triangular off-diagonal block into a matrix:

$$
\mathcal {c} (\boldsymbol {m}) = \left[ \left[ \boldsymbol {m} \right] _ {\mathcal {z} (1)} ^ {v}, \dots , \left[ \boldsymbol {m} \right] _ {\mathcal {z} (l)} ^ {v} \right] ^ {\intercal}. \tag {92}
$$

given a solution  $\hat{\pmb{x}} = [\hat{\pmb{q}}^{\mathsf{t}},\hat{\theta}_{1}\hat{\pmb{q}}^{\mathsf{t}},\dots ,\hat{\theta}_{k}\hat{\pmb{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}]^{\mathsf{t}}$  to problem (p), denote  $\hat{\pmb{r}}$  to be the unique rotation matrix corresponding to  $\hat{\pmb{q}}$  and define the following residual vectors:

$$
\hat {\boldsymbol {\xi}} _ {k} = \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} ^ {\mathsf {t}} \left(\bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {k} - \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {k}\right), k = 1, \dots , k, \tag {93}
$$

for each pair of tims  $(\bar{a}_k,\bar{b}_k)$  (scale  $\bar{a}_k$  by:  $\bar{a}_k\gets \hat{s}\bar{a}_k$

proposition 33 (projection onto  $\bar{\mathcal{l}}$ ). given any matrix  $m \in s^{4(k+1)}$ , its projection onto  $\bar{\mathcal{l}}$ , denoted  $\tilde{m} \doteq \pi_{\bar{\mathcal{l}}}(\bar{m})$ , is:

$$
\bar {m} = \pi_ {\bar {\mathcal {h}}} (\boldsymbol {h}) + \bar {\boldsymbol {q}} - \hat {\mu} \boldsymbol {j}, \tag {94}
$$

where  $\pmb{h} \doteq \pmb{m} - \bar{\pmb{q}} + \hat{\mu}\pmb{j}$  and  $\bar{\pmb{h}} \doteq \pi_{\bar{\mathcal{h}}}(\pmb{h})$  can be computed as follows:

1) project the matrix part of each diagonal block:

$$
[ \bar {\boldsymbol {h}} ] _ {k k} ^ {m} = [ \boldsymbol {h} ] _ {k k} ^ {m} - \frac {\sum_ {k = 0} ^ {k} [ \boldsymbol {h} ] _ {k k} ^ {m}}{k + 1}, \forall k = 0, \dots , k. \tag {95}
$$

2) project the scalar part of each diagonal block:

$$
[ \bar {\boldsymbol {h}} ] _ {k k} ^ {s} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} - \left(\frac {1}{4} \hat {\theta} _ {k} + \frac {1}{2}\right) \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\xi}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} + \left(\frac {1}{4} \hat {\theta} _ {k} - \frac {1}{2}\right) \bar {c} ^ {2} & k \neq 0 \\ \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {k} \left(\frac {1}{4} \hat {\theta} _ {k} + \frac {1}{2}\right) \| \hat {\boldsymbol {\xi}} _ {k} \| ^ {2} - \left(\frac {1}{4} \hat {\theta} _ {k} - \frac {1}{2}\right) \bar {c} ^ {2} & k = 0. \end{array} \right. \tag {96}
$$

3) project the matrix part of each off-diagonal block:

$$
[ \bar {\boldsymbol {h}} ] _ {\mathcal {z} (l)} ^ {m} = \frac {[ \boldsymbol {h} ] _ {\mathcal {z} (l)} ^ {m} - ([ \boldsymbol {h} ] _ {\mathcal {z} (l)} ^ {m}) ^ {\intercal}}{2}, \forall l = 1, \dots , l. \tag {97}
$$

4) project the scalar part of each off-diagonal block:

$$
[ \bar {h} ] _ {\mathcal {z} (l)} ^ {s} = 0, \forall l = 1, \dots , l. \tag {98}
$$

5) project the vector part of each off-diagonal block:

$$
\mathcal {c} (\bar {\boldsymbol {h}}) = \boldsymbol {p} \cdot \mathcal {f} (\boldsymbol {h}), \tag {99}
$$

where  $\pmb{p} \in \mathcal{s}^l$  and  $\mathcal{f}(\pmb{h}) \in \mathbb{r}^{l \times 3}$  are defined in appendix  $u$ .

6) project the vector part of each diagonal block using the results of step 5:

$$
[ \bar {h} ] _ {k k} ^ {v} = - \sum_ {i = 0, i \neq k} ^ {k} \hat {\theta} _ {k} \hat {\theta} _ {i} [ \bar {h} ] _ {k i} ^ {v} + \phi_ {k}, \tag {100}
$$

$$
\phi_ {k} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} - \left(\frac {1}{2} \hat {\theta} _ {k} + 1\right) \left[ \hat {\xi} _ {k} \right] _ {\times} \bar {a} _ {k} & k = 1, \dots , k \\ \sum_ {k = 1} ^ {k} \left(\frac {1}{2} \hat {\theta} _ {k} + 1\right) \left[ \hat {\xi} _ {k} \right] _ {\times} \bar {a} _ {k} & k = 0 \end{array} \right. \tag {101}
$$

a complete proof of proposition 33 is algebraically involved and is given in appendix u.

n. proof of theorem 15: estimation contract with noiseless inliers and random outliers

we prove theorem 15 in two steps. first we show that under assumptions (iii)-(iv) of the theorem, the inliers found by teaser match the maximum consensus solution. then, we prove that under assumptions (i)-(ii) the maximum consensus solution is unique and recovers the ground truth, from which the claims of the theorem follow.

1) tls ≡ mc: let the number of inliers in the maximum consensus be  $n_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{mc}}$ , since the inliers are noise-free, the sum of the squared residual errors in the maximum consensus set,  $r_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{mc}}$ , must be zero. by lemma 23, if tls selects a different consensus set than the maximum consensus set, then the consensus set that tls selects must have size  $n_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{tls}}$  greater than  $n_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{mc}} - r_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{mc}} / \bar{c}^{2}$ , i.e.,  $n_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{tls}} > n_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{mc}} - r_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{mc}} / \bar{c}^{2} = n_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{mc}}$ , contradicting the fact that  $n_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{mc}}$  is the size of the maximum consensus set. therefore, tls ≡ mc for each subproblem.

2) exact recovery of maximum consensus: we now prove that maximum consensus recovers the ground truth under the assumptions of the theorem. let us start with the scale subproblem. the inliers are noiseless and distinct (assumption (i) in the theorem), so each pair of inliers will produce a trim  $s_k = s^\circ$ . since we have at least 3 inliers, there are at least 3 such trims. since the outliers are in generic position (assumption (ii) in the theorem), the event that more than 3 trims formed by the outliers will have the same scale estimate  $\bar{s}$  that is different from  $s^\circ$  happens with probability zero. therefore, the maximum consensus solution coincides with the ground-truth scale. the same logic can be repeated for the rotation and translation subproblems, leading to identical conclusions under the assumption that the rotation subproblem finds a certifiably optimal tls estimate (condition (iv)).

3) exact recovery of teaser: since under the assumptions of the theorem, teaser matches the maximum consensus solution in each subproblem, and maximum consensus recovers the ground truth in each subproblem with probability 1, teaser almost surely recovers the ground-truth transformation, i.e.,  $\hat{s} = s^{\circ}$ ,  $\hat{r} = r^{\circ}$ ,  $\hat{t} = t^{\circ}$ , proving the theorem.

o. proof of theorem 16: estimation contract with noiseless inliers and adversarial outliers

as for theorem 15, we prove theorem 16 in two steps.

1) tls ≡ mc: the proof of this part is identical to the corresponding proof in theorem 15.  
2) exact recovery of maximum consensus: different than the case with random outliers, we need condition (i), a stronger assumption on the number of inliers to guarantee recovery of the ground truth. let the ground-truth consensus set be  $\mathcal{c}^{\circ}$  whose size is at least  $n_{\mathrm{in}}$  (the outliers can accidentally become inliers) in condition (i). suppose an adversary wants to trick mc to return a solution  $(\bar{s},\bar{r},\bar{t})$  that is different from the ground truth, s/he must have a consensus set,  $\bar{c}$ , of size  $\bar{n}_{\mathrm{in}}$  that is no smaller than  $n_{\mathrm{in}}$ , in which the measurements are consistent w.r.t.  $(\bar{s},\bar{r},\bar{t})$ . now because the total number of measurement is  $n_{\mathrm{in}} + n_{\mathrm{out}}$ , and both  $\mathcal{c}^{\circ}$  and  $\bar{c}$  have size at least  $n_{\mathrm{in}}$ , this means  $\mathcal{c}^{\circ}$  and  $\bar{c}$  must share at least  $2n_{\mathrm{in}} - (n_{\mathrm{in}} + n_{\mathrm{out}}) = n_{\mathrm{in}} - n_{\mathrm{out}}$  common correspondences.

from condition (i), we know that  $n_{\mathrm{in}} - n_{\mathrm{out}} \geq 3$ . however, if  $c^\circ$  and  $\bar{c}$  share at least 3 common noise-free correspondences, then  $(\bar{s}, \bar{\pmb{r}}, \bar{t}) \equiv (s^\circ, \pmb{r}^\circ, t^\circ)$  must be true. therefore, we conclude that there is no chance for the adversary to trick mc, and mc must recover the ground-truth transformation when condition (i) holds.

3) exact recovery of teaser: condition (iv) guarantees that teaser returned a consensus set that satisfies condition (i). however, according to section o2 only the ground truth can produce an estimate that satisfies condition (i), hence teaser's solution must match the ground truth, proving the theorem. note that recovering the ground truth does not guarantee that all outliers are rejected, since in the adversarial regime outliers can be indistinguishable from inliers (i.e., an outlier  $i$  can satisfy  $\boldsymbol{b}_i = s^\circ \boldsymbol{r}^\circ \boldsymbol{a}_i + t^\circ$ ), a fact that, however, does not have negative repercussions on the accuracy of teaser.

# p. proof of theorem 17: estimation contract with noisy inliers and adversarial outliers

we prove theorem 17 in two steps. first we show that under assumptions (ii) and (iii) of the theorem, the inliers produced by teaser match the maximum consensus set and contain the set of true inliers. then, we prove that under assumption (i) and (iv) the maximum consensus solution is also close to the ground truth, in the sense of inequalities (26)-(28).

1) tls ≡ mc and inliers are preserved: we prove that under assumptions (ii) and (iii), the inliers produced by teaser match the maximum consensus set and contain the set of true inliers. in each subproblem, lemma 23 and assumption (iii) guarantee that teaser computes a maximum consensus set. assumption (iii) implies the maximum consensus set contains the inliers (and hence teaser preserves the inliers).

2) noisy recovery of maximum consensus: we now prove that maximum consensus produces a solution "close" to the ground truth under the assumptions of the theorem. let us start with the scale subproblem. assumption (ii) in the theorem ensures that the maximum consensus set contains all inliers in each subproblem. let us now derive the bounds on the resulting estimate, starting from the scale estimation.

scale error bound. since the scale estimation selects all the inliers, plus potentially some outliers, it holds for some of the selected measurements  $i,j$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| s ^ {\circ} - \hat {s} \right| = \left| s ^ {\circ} - \hat {s} + \epsilon_ {k} ^ {s} - \epsilon_ {k} ^ {s} \right| (102) \\ \stackrel {(a)} {\leq} \left| s ^ {\circ} + \epsilon_ {k} ^ {s} - \hat {s} \right| + \left| \epsilon_ {k} ^ {s} \right| (103) \\ \stackrel {(b)} {\leq} \left| s _ {k} - \hat {s} \right| + \alpha_ {i j} \stackrel {(c)} {\leq} 2 \alpha_ {i j} (104) \\ \end{array}
$$

where in (a) we used the triangle inequality, in (b) we noticed that for inliers  $i,j$ , it holds  $|\epsilon_k^s |\leq \alpha_{ij}$  and  $s^\circ +\epsilon_k^s = s_k$ , and in (c) we noticed that for  $s_k$  to be considered an inlier by tls,  $|s_k - \hat{s} |\leq \alpha_{ij}$ . since the inequality has to hold for all the true inliers, but we do not know which ones of the measurements selected by tls are true inliers, we substitute the bound with:

$$
\left| s ^ {\circ} - \hat {s} \right| \leq 2 \max  _ {i j} \alpha_ {i j} \tag {105}
$$

which proves the first bound in (26).

rotation error bound. let us now move to rotation estimation. since the rotation subproblem also selects all the inliers, plus potentially some outliers, it holds for some of the selected measurements  $i,j$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \| s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {i j} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {i j} \| = \| s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {i j} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {i j} + \epsilon_ {i j} - \epsilon_ {i j} \| (1 0 6) \\ \stackrel {(a)} {=} \| \bar {\boldsymbol {b}} _ {i j} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {i j} - \epsilon_ {i j} \| (1 0 7) \\ \stackrel {(b)} {\leq} \left\| \bar {b} _ {i j} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {i j} \right\| + \left\| \boldsymbol {\epsilon} _ {i j} \right\| \stackrel {(c)} {\leq} 2 \delta_ {i j} (1 0 8) \\ \end{array}
$$

where in (a) we noticed that for inliers  $i,j$ ,  $\bar{\pmb{b}}_{ij} = s^{\circ}\pmb{r}^{\circ}\bar{\pmb{a}}_{ij} + \pmb{\epsilon}_{ij}$ , in (b) we used the triangle inequality, and in (c) we noticed that it holds  $\| \pmb{\epsilon}_{ij}\| \leq \delta_{ij}$  and that for  $(i,j)$  to be considered an inlier by tls,  $\| \bar{\pmb{b}}_{ij} - \hat{s}\hat{r}\bar{\pmb{a}}_{ij}\| \leq \delta_{ij}$ .

squaring the inequality and defining  $\pmb{u}_{ij} \doteq \frac{\bar{a}_{ij}}{\|\bar{a}_{ij}\|}$ :

$$
\left\| s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} \bar {a} _ {i j} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {i j} \right\| ^ {2} \leq 4 \delta_ {i j} ^ {2} \longleftrightarrow \tag {109}
$$

$$
\left\| s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {u} _ {i j} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \boldsymbol {u} _ {i j} \right\| ^ {2} \leq 4 \frac {\delta_ {i j} ^ {2}}{\| \bar {\boldsymbol {a}} _ {i j} \| ^ {2}} = 4 \alpha_ {i j} ^ {2} \tag {110}
$$

since we have at least 4 inliers  $i, j, h, k$ , whose invariant measurements include  $\bar{a}_{ij}, \bar{a}_{ih}, \bar{a}_{ik}$ , and each invariant measurement satisfies (110), we can sum (member-wise) the 3 corresponding inequalities and obtain:

$$
\left\| s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {u} _ {i j} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \boldsymbol {u} _ {i j} \right\| ^ {2} + \left\| s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {u} _ {i h} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \boldsymbol {u} _ {i h} \right\| ^ {2} + \tag {111}
$$

$$
\left\| s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {u} _ {i k} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \boldsymbol {u} _ {i k} \right\| ^ {2} \leq 1 2 \alpha_ {i j} ^ {2} \tag {112}
$$

$$
\longleftrightarrow \| \left(s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}}\right) u _ {i j h k} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \leq 1 2 \alpha_ {i j} ^ {2} \tag {113}
$$

where  $\mathbf{u}_{ijk} = [\mathbf{u}_{ij}\mathbf{u}_{ih}\mathbf{u}_{ik}]$  and matches the definition in the statement of the theorem. now we note that for any two matrices  $\mathbf{a}$  and  $\mathbf{b}$  it holds  $\| \mathbf{ab}\|_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}\geq \sigma_{\min}(\mathbf{b})^{2}\| \mathbf{a}\|_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}$ , which applied to (113) becomes:

$$
\left\| \left(s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}}\right) \boldsymbol {u} _ {i j h k} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \geq \tag {114}
$$

$$
\sigma_ {\min } \left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {i j h k}\right) ^ {2} \| s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \tag {115}
$$

chaining the inequality (115) back into (113):

$$
\sigma_ {\min } \left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {i j h k}\right) ^ {2} \| s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \| _ {\mathrm {f}} ^ {2} \leq 1 2 \alpha_ {i j} ^ {2} \longleftrightarrow \tag {116}
$$

$$
\left\| s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq 2 \sqrt {3} \frac {\alpha_ {i j}}{\sigma_ {\min } (\boldsymbol {u} _ {i j h k})} \tag {117}
$$

since we do not know what are the true inliers, we have to take the worst case over all possible  $i,j,h,k$  in (117), yielding:

$$
\left\| s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} - \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \right\| _ {\mathrm {f}} \leq 2 \sqrt {3} \frac {\operatorname* {m a x} _ {i j} \alpha_ {i j}}{\min  _ {i j h k} \sigma_ {\min } \left(\boldsymbol {u} _ {i j h k}\right)} \tag {118}
$$

we finally observe that  $\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}(u_{ijk})$  is different from zero as long as the vectors  $\pmb{u}_{ij}, \pmb{u}_{ih}, \pmb{u}_{ik}$  are linearly independent (i.e., do not lie in the same plane), which is the case when the four inliers are not coplanar. this proves the second bound in (27).

translation error bound. since the translation subproblem also selects all the inliers, plus potentially some outliers, it holds for each inlier point  $i$ :

$$
\boldsymbol {b} _ {i} = s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} + t ^ {\circ} + \epsilon_ {i} \quad \text {a n d} \tag {119}
$$

$$
\boldsymbol {b} _ {i} = \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} + \hat {\boldsymbol {t}} + \phi_ {i} \quad \text {w i t h} \| \phi_ {i} \| \leq \sqrt {3} \beta_ {i}
$$

where the first follows from the definition of inlier, and the second from the maximum error of a measurement considered inlier by tls (the factor  $\sqrt{3}$  comes from the fact

that translation is estimated component-wise, which means  $\| \phi_i\| ^2 = [\phi_i]_1^2 +[\phi_i]_2^2 +[\phi_i]_3^2\leq 3\beta_i^2$ . combining the equalities (119):

$$
\begin{array}{l} \hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} + \hat {\boldsymbol {t}} + \phi_ {i} = s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} + \boldsymbol {t} ^ {\circ} + \epsilon_ {i} \tag {120} \\ \longleftrightarrow \boldsymbol {t} ^ {\circ} - \hat {\boldsymbol {t}} = (\hat {s} \boldsymbol {r} - s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ}) \boldsymbol {a} _ {i} + \left(\phi_ {i} - \epsilon_ {i}\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

since we have at least 4 inliers  $i, j, h, k$ , each satisfying the equality (120), we subtract (member-wise) the first equality (including inlier  $i$ ) from the sum of the last 3 equalities (including points  $j, h, k$ ):

$$
\begin{array}{l} 2 \left(\boldsymbol {t} ^ {\circ} - \hat {\boldsymbol {t}}\right) = \sum_ {l \in \{j, h, k \}} \left(\hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} - s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ}\right) \left(\boldsymbol {a} _ {l} - \boldsymbol {a} _ {i}\right) (121) \\ + \sum_ {l \in \{j, h, k \}} \left(\phi_ {l} - \epsilon_ {l} - \phi_ {i} + \epsilon_ {i}\right) (122) \\ \end{array}
$$

taking the norm of both members and using the triangle inequality:

$$
\begin{array}{l} 2 \| \boldsymbol {t} ^ {\circ} - \hat {\boldsymbol {t}} \| \leq \sum_ {l \in \{j, h, k \}} \| (\hat {s} \hat {\boldsymbol {r}} - s ^ {\circ} \boldsymbol {r} ^ {\circ}) (\boldsymbol {a} _ {l} - \boldsymbol {a} _ {i}) \| \tag {123} \\ + \sum_ {l \in \{j, h, k \}} \left(\| \phi_ {l} \| + \| \epsilon_ {l} \| + \| \phi_ {i} \| + \| \epsilon_ {i} \|\right) (1 2 4) \\ \end{array}
$$

noting that  $\| (\hat{s}\hat{\pmb{r}} -s^{\circ}\pmb{r}^{\circ})(\pmb{a}_{l} - \pmb{a}_{i})\| = \| (\hat{s}\hat{\pmb{r}} -s^{\circ}\pmb{r}^{\circ})\bar{\pmb{a}}_{il}\|$  must satisfy the inequality (108), and the last 4 terms in (124) have norm bounded by  $\beta$  and  $\sqrt{3}\beta$ :

$$
2 \| \boldsymbol {t} ^ {\circ} - \hat {\boldsymbol {t}} \| \leq \sum_ {l \in \{j, h, k \}} 2 \delta_ {i j} + \sum_ {l \in \{j, h, k \}} (2 + 2 \sqrt {3}) \beta \tag {125}
$$

since we assume all the points to have the same noise bound  $\beta$ , it holds  $\delta_{ij} = 2\beta$  and the right-hand-side of (125) becomes  $(18 + 6\sqrt{3})\beta$ , from which it follows:

$$
2 \| \boldsymbol {t} ^ {\circ} - \hat {\boldsymbol {t}} \| \leq (1 8 + 6 \sqrt {3}) \beta \iff \| \boldsymbol {t} ^ {\circ} - \hat {\boldsymbol {t}} \| \leq (9 + 3 \sqrt {3}) \beta \tag {126}
$$

which proves the last bound in (28).

# q. tls translation estimation: component-wise vs. full

component-wise translation estimation is a simple approximation for the full translation estimate. the full tls translation estimate can be obtained using techniques described in [94], [35]. in this section, we provide numerical experiments to show that the component-wise translation estimation is sufficiently close to the full tls translation estimate.

the tls estimator of the full translation vector  $\pmb{t} \in \mathbb{r}^3$ , given noisy and outlying observations  $\pmb{t}_i, i = 1, \dots, n$ , is the globally optimal solution of the following non-convex optimization:

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {t} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {3}} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \min  \left(\frac {\| \boldsymbol {t} - \boldsymbol {t} _ {i} \| ^ {2}}{\beta_ {i} ^ {2}}, \bar {c} ^ {2}\right), \tag {127}
$$

where  $\pmb{t}_i = \pmb{b}_i - \hat{s}\hat{\pmb{r}}\pmb{a}_i$  in the context of teaser (cf. eq. (13)). as presented in section vi-c, instead of estimating the full translation by solving problem (127), teaser seeks to estimate the translation vector component-wise by leveraging the adaptive voting algorithm for solving the scalar tls problem. in this section, we show that component-wise translation estimation is sufficiently close to the full translation estimation.

![](images/926af732ebf423752262ec852ff5562bd6e81a1568953be1528da74c5064c262.jpg)  
fig. 12. comparison of four robust translation estimation methods.

in order to show the accuracy of component-wise translation estimation, we design two methods to solve problem (127) globally. the two methods bear great similarity to the semidefinite relaxation [39] and graduated non-convexity (gnc) algorithms [34] presented for solving the rotation subproblem. (i) (tls: sdp) we use lasserre's hierarchy of moment relaxations [123] to relax the nonconvex problem into a convex sdp. by solving the sdp, we can obtain an estimate of the full translation, together with a global optimality certificate. this method has similar flavor as to the sdp relaxation we presented for estimating rotation, but is slightly more involved. the interested reader can refer to [35] for more technical details. (i) (tls: gnc) we use gnc to solve the tls translation estimation problem, where the non-minimal solver is simply the weighted average of the noisy measurements  $t_i$ 's. gnc is very efficient but it offers no optimality guarantees. comparison of these two methods (for solving the full translation) with adaptive voting (for solving the component-wise translation) are shown in fig. 12 (using the same experimental setup as in section xi-a). we can see that: (i) tls: sdp performs the best. it is robust to  $90\%$  outliers and returns the most accurate translation estimates. the estimates of tls: sdp are indeed the globally optimal solutions, because the relaxation is empirically always tight (duality gap is zero). (ii) as expected, solving the translation component-wise is an approximation, and it is slightly less accurate than solving the whole translation. however, the estimates are sufficiently close to the globally optimal solutions (when the outlier rate is below  $90\%$ ). (iii) the gnc method obtains the globally optimal solution when the outlier rate is below  $90\%$ . this is similar to the performance of the gnc method for solving the rotation subproblem presented in teaser.

# r. sdp relaxations:quaternion vs. rotation matrix

in section xi, we compare the rotation estimation error between the quaternion-based sdp relaxation (eq. (23), [39]) and the rotation-matrix-based sdp relaxation ([38]) on the bunny dataset. in this section, we also report the relative duality gap for the two relaxations on the bunny dataset. fig. 13(a) shows the relative duality gap (boxes in red, scale on the right-hand y-axis) and the rotation error (boxes in blue, scale on the left-hand y-axis). the figure shows that the quaternion-based relaxation is always tighter than the rotation-matrix-

![](images/bc2bbdfecb7f93deaa4fd493a9401c87296b49216fdf1d4c5411c9348422a9a7.jpg)

![](images/1099f27e9efada8b57400fc5c451eefd4ca33a9275960d3e445eb2c673ad4358.jpg)  
(a) results on the bunny dataset.  
(b) results on random simulated unit vectors.  
fig. 13. comparison of rotation errors (left-axis, blue) and relative duality gap (right-axis, red) between the quaternion-based sdp relaxation (filled boxplots) and the rotation-matrix-based sdp relaxation (empty boxplots) on (a) the bunny dataset and (b) random simulated unit vectors.

based relaxation, although both relaxations achieve similar performance in terms of rotation estimation errors.

to further stress-test the two techniques, we compare their performance in a more randomized test setting [39]: at each monte carlo run, we first randomly sample  $k = 40$  unit vectors from the 3d unit sphere, then apply a random rotation and isotropic gaussian noise (standard deviation  $\sigma = 0.01$ ) to the  $k$  unit vectors. fig. 13(b) shows the results under this setting. we can see that the quaternion-based relaxation remains tight, while the rotation-matrix-based relaxation has a much larger relative duality gap. in addition, a tighter relaxation translates to more accurate rotation estimation: the quaternion-based relaxation dominates the rotation-matrix relaxation and obtains much more accurate rotation estimates, especially at  $>80\%$  outlier rates.

# s. stanford dataset: extra results

in all the following experiments,  $\bar{c}^2 = 1$  for scale, rotation and translation estimation.

1) benchmark on synthetic datasets: as stated in section xi-b in the main document, we have benchmarked teaser against four state-of-the-art methods in point cloud registration (fgr [55], gore [19], ransac1k, ransac) at increasing level of outliers, using four datasets from the stanford 3d scanning repository [97]: bunny, armadillo, dragon

and buddha. results for the bunny are showed in the main document and here we show the results for the other three datasets in fig. 14. the figure confirms that teaser dominates the other techniques is insensitive to extreme outlier rates.

2) teaser on high noise: in the main document, the standard deviation  $\sigma$  of the isotropic gaussian noise is set to be 0.01, with the point cloud scaled inside a unit cube  $[0,1]^3$ . in this section, we give a visual illustration of how corrupted the point cloud is after adding noise of such magnitude. in addition, we increase the noise standard deviation  $\sigma$  to be 0.1, visualize the corresponding corrupted point cloud, and show that teaser can still recover the ground-truth transformation with reasonable accuracy. fig. 17 illustrates a clean (noise-free) bunny model (fig. 17(a)) scaled inside the unit cube and its variants by adding different levels of isotropic gaussian noise and outliers. from fig. 17(b)(c), we can see  $\sigma = 0.01$  (noise used in the main document) is a reasonable noise standard deviation for real-world applications, while  $\sigma = 0.1$  destroys the geometric structure of the bunny and it is beyond the noise typically encountered in robotics and computer vision applications. fig. 17(d) shows the bunny with high noise  $(\sigma = 0.1)$  and  $50\%$  outliers. under this setup, we run teaser to register the two point clouds and recover the relative transformation. teaser can still return rotation and translation estimates that are close to the ground-truth transformation. however, due to the severe noise corruption, even a successful registration fails to yield a visually convincing registration result to human perception. for example, fig. 15 shows a representative teaser registration result with  $\sigma = 0.1$  noise corruption and  $50\%$  outlier rate, where accurate transformation estimates are obtained (rotation error is  $3.42^{\circ}$  and translation error is  $0.098\mathrm{m}$ ), but the bunny in the cluttered scene is hardly visible, even when super-imposed to the clean model.  
3) teaser++ on problems with increasing number of correspondences: here we provide more results for teaser++ on problems with higher number of correspondences. we follow the same experimental setup as detailed in section xi-b, with the exception that we fixed our outlier rate at  $95\%$ , and test the algorithms with increasing numbers of correspondences. fig. 16 shows the results of the experiments.

overall, in terms of rotation errors and translation errors,  $\mathrm{teaser}^{++}$  dominates other methods by a large margin. in terms of speed,  $\mathrm{teaser}^{++}$  outperforms other robust estimation algorithms such as gore. while  $\mathrm{teaser}^{++}$ 's runtime scales with the number of correspondences, it still outperforms methods such as gore on all cases. at 10000 correspondences,  $\mathrm{teaser}^{++}$  only takes around 2 seconds to solve the registration problem. this demonstrates that  $\mathrm{teaser}^{++}$  can be used to solve registration problems with high number of correspondences within reasonable amount of time.

# t. object pose estimation: extra results
